## Needs a real cook or a specialist

- **cooking** [macarons](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/macarons/): /recipes/macarons/ (src/data/details/french.js): shell cooling and ganache firming
  - Problem: No duration can be given honestly from the text.
  - Done: Left as untimed stages with readiness cues; the page states the total as a minimum.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: Needs one real bake, timed: shells from oven to clean release, ganache from fridge to pipeable.
- **specialist** [aguachile](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/aguachile/): 500 g raw prawns, peeled and butterflied
  - Problem: Raw prawns served "cooked" only in lime juice; acid does not kill bacteria or parasites.
  - Done: Left as written and listed for a specialist in src/data/food-handling-reviewed.json; no method change makes raw prawns safe.
  - Source: FDA: cook seafood; acid marinades do not make raw seafood safe.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a specialist decision on whether to keep the dish and what to tell readers who are pregnant, elderly, very young or immunocompromised; the owner decides.
- **specialist** [steak-tartare](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/steak-tartare/): 600 g beef fillet or rump, very fresh, all sinew removed
  - Problem: Raw beef, served uncooked by definition.
  - Done: The yolks are now pasteurised (separate record). The beef is left as written: whole-muscle, hand-cut, kept cold, eaten within the hour. Listed for a specialist in src/data/food-handling-reviewed.json.
  - Source: USDA FSIS safe minimum temperatures: whole beef cuts 63°C / 145°F, minced beef 71°C / 160°F.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a specialist decision on the wording for vulnerable readers; the owner decides.
- **specialist** [carpaccio](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/carpaccio/): 400 g beef fillet, in one piece, all sinew removed
  - Problem: Raw beef, served uncooked by definition.
  - Done: The yolk in the sauce is now pasteurised (separate record). The beef is left as written and listed for a specialist in src/data/food-handling-reviewed.json.
  - Source: USDA FSIS safe minimum temperatures: whole beef cuts 63°C / 145°F.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a specialist decision on the wording for vulnerable readers; the owner decides.
- **specialist** [makdous](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/makdous/): Vegetables cured and kept under oil for weeks (corrected to the fridge from the day it is made, with no keeping time, in commit 0f8d674e).
  - Problem: The method cures vegetables under oil for weeks, which the 4-day oil guidance cannot accommodate; a 4-day limit would make the dish impossible as written.
  - Done: Storage moved to the fridge with an honest note that no keeping time has been tested and that botulism cannot be seen, smelled or tasted; the method itself is kept and listed for a specialist.
  - Source: NCHFP (National Center for Home Food Preservation), "Freezing Garlic-in-Oil": garlic-in-oil made fresh, refrigerated at 4°C / 40°F or lower for no more than 4 days, or frozen. Oregon State University Extension SP 50-701, "Herbs and vegetables in oil" (rev. January 2025): fresh or dried garlic and herbs in oil, pesto, and dried tomatoes seasoned with garlic or herbs must be refrigerated and used within 4 days, or frozen. OSU SP 50-677, "Guidelines for refrigeration of foods" (rev. July 2024): flavoured oils and pesto not more than 4 days. CDC: botulinum toxin cannot be seen, smelled or tasted.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a tested process for this preserve (pH and salt measured, or a validated acidification), from a food-safety specialist.
- **specialist** [betengan-mekhalel](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/betengan-mekhalel/): Vegetables cured and kept under oil for weeks (corrected to the fridge from the day it is made, with no keeping time, in commit 0f8d674e).
  - Problem: The method cures vegetables under oil for weeks, which the 4-day oil guidance cannot accommodate; a 4-day limit would make the dish impossible as written.
  - Done: Storage moved to the fridge with an honest note that no keeping time has been tested and that botulism cannot be seen, smelled or tasted; the method itself is kept and listed for a specialist.
  - Source: NCHFP (National Center for Home Food Preservation), "Freezing Garlic-in-Oil": garlic-in-oil made fresh, refrigerated at 4°C / 40°F or lower for no more than 4 days, or frozen. Oregon State University Extension SP 50-701, "Herbs and vegetables in oil" (rev. January 2025): fresh or dried garlic and herbs in oil, pesto, and dried tomatoes seasoned with garlic or herbs must be refrigerated and used within 4 days, or frozen. OSU SP 50-677, "Guidelines for refrigeration of foods" (rev. July 2024): flavoured oils and pesto not more than 4 days. CDC: botulinum toxin cannot be seen, smelled or tasted.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a tested process for this preserve (pH and salt measured, or a validated acidification), from a food-safety specialist.
- **specialist** [giardiniera](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/giardiniera/): Vegetables cured and kept under oil for weeks (corrected to the fridge from the day it is made, with no keeping time, in commit 0f8d674e).
  - Problem: The method cures vegetables under oil for weeks, which the 4-day oil guidance cannot accommodate; a 4-day limit would make the dish impossible as written.
  - Done: Storage moved to the fridge with an honest note that no keeping time has been tested and that botulism cannot be seen, smelled or tasted; the method itself is kept and listed for a specialist.
  - Source: NCHFP (National Center for Home Food Preservation), "Freezing Garlic-in-Oil": garlic-in-oil made fresh, refrigerated at 4°C / 40°F or lower for no more than 4 days, or frozen. Oregon State University Extension SP 50-701, "Herbs and vegetables in oil" (rev. January 2025): fresh or dried garlic and herbs in oil, pesto, and dried tomatoes seasoned with garlic or herbs must be refrigerated and used within 4 days, or frozen. OSU SP 50-677, "Guidelines for refrigeration of foods" (rev. July 2024): flavoured oils and pesto not more than 4 days. CDC: botulinum toxin cannot be seen, smelled or tasted.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a tested process for this preserve (pH and salt measured, or a validated acidification), from a food-safety specialist.
- **specialist** [miso-ramen](https://weeklydelight.com/recipes/miso-ramen/): Tare keeps 3 weeks refrigerated (miso, mirin, soy, sesame paste, raw grated garlic and ginger).
  - Problem: Raw garlic and ginger held for 3 weeks in a salty paste; the oil guidance does not cover a miso paste, and no home guidance gives a keeping time for it.
  - Done: Left as written and listed for a specialist.
  - Source: NCHFP and OSU guidance cover garlic in oil, not garlic in a high-salt paste; none was found for this case.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: salt and water-activity figures for the tare, or specialist review.
- **specialist** catalogue/leftover-lives: 717 recipes still give fridge lives over 4 days after this pass (stews, braises, sauces, salads, cakes, pickles); the list is docs/release-2026-10-uncertainties/food-long-fridge-lives.md.
  - Problem: USDA FSIS gives 3 to 4 days for cooked leftovers; many recipes give 5 days or more. The brief rules out applying one universal time, and many of the 732 are foods the leftover guidance does not cover (pickles, cakes, jams, cured foods).
  - Done: Not changed in bulk. Only the oil preserves, meat under fat and jars, where specific guidance exists, were corrected.
  - Source: USDA FSIS, "Leftovers and Food Safety": cooked leftovers keep 3 to 4 days in the refrigerator; freeze for longer; reheat to 74°C / 165°F.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: a per-category shelf-life policy from a food-safety specialist, then applied recipe by recipe.
- **specialist** catalogue/runny-yolks: Poached, fried and soft-boiled eggs with runny yolks across the catalogue (eggs Benedict, ramen eggs, shakshuka, fried-egg sandwiches and others).
  - Problem: FSIS advises cooking eggs until yolks and whites are firm, or using pasteurised eggs; the UK FSA allows runny Lion eggs for everyone except the severely immunocompromised.
  - Done: Not changed: runny yolks are how these dishes are made, and the guidance differs by country. Raw-egg sauces and foams, which FDA and FSIS name, were corrected.
  - Source: FDA, egg safety for consumers: use pasteurised eggs or egg products in dishes made with raw or undercooked eggs; egg dishes are safe at 71°C / 160°F. USDA FSIS, "Shell Eggs from Farm to Table": Caesar dressing, hollandaise and béarnaise, mayonnaise, eggnog, mousse and other uncooked egg dishes are made with pasteurised eggs. UK Food Standards Agency (2017): British Lion eggs can be eaten raw or lightly cooked by vulnerable groups, except the severely immunocompromised.
  - Missing evidence or limitation: an editorial and specialist decision on whether runny-yolk dishes should name pasteurised or Lion-stamped eggs.
